Verify each numbered key claim against the solution text, identify the central technique, and assign a rigor score per the audit rubric.\n     8\t\n     9\t## Problem\n    10\t\n    11\tLet \\(n\\) be a positive integer and let \\(a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_k\\) (\\(k \\geq 2\\)) be distinct integers in the set \\(\\{1, 2, \\ldots, n\\}\\) such that \\(n\\) divides \\(a_i(a_{i+1} - 1)\\) for \\(i = 1, 2, \\ldots, k-1\\). Prove that \\(n\\) does not divide \\(a_k(a_1 - 1)\\).\n    12\t\n    13\t## Full Published Solution\n    14\t\n    15\t```text\n    16\tStep 1.  Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that n divides a_k(a_1 - 1).  Combined with\n    17\tthe hypothesis n | a_i(a_{i+1} - 1) for i = 1, ..., k-1, this gives n | a_i(a_{i+1} - 1) for\n    18\tALL indices i mod k (with subscripts taken cyclically).\n    19\t\n    20\tStep 2.  Let p be any prime divisor of n.  We work modulo p^v where v = v_p(n) is the p-adic\n    21\tvaluation of n.  Define b_i = v_p(a_i) for each i.\n    22\t\n    23\tStep 3.  From p^v | a_i (a_{i+1} - 1), we get p^v | a_i · (a_{i+1} - 1).  Since v_p(a_i) = b_i\n    24\tand v_p(a_{i+1} - 1) = v - b_i (taking the cyclic relation), we conclude b_i + (v - b_i) = v,\n    25\twhich is automatic and gives no new information.\n    26\t\n    27\tStep 4.  Instead, look at the quantity gcd(a_i, n).  We claim gcd(a_i, n) is constant in i,\n    28\tmodulo the cyclic indexing.  Indeed if g_i = gcd(a_i, n), then g_i | n and g_i | a_i(a_{i+1}-1).\n    29\tSince gcd(a_i, a_{i+1} - 1) is constrained by the cyclic divisibility chain, by Bezout's lemma\n    30\tg_i divides g_{i+1}.  Cyclically, g_1 | g_2 | ... | g_k | g_1, forcing all g_i equal.\n    31\t\n    32\tStep 5.  Let g be this common value.  Then a_i = g · m_i with gcd(m_i, n/g) = 1.  The relation\n    33\tn | a_i(a_{i+1} - 1) becomes (n/g) | a_{i+1} - 1 (after dividing by g and using coprimality of\n    34\tm_i with n/g).  Hence a_{i+1} ≡ 1 (mod n/g) for every i.\n    35\t\n    36\tStep 6.  Cyclically, a_1 ≡ 1 (mod n/g).  But a_1 is distinct from 1 (otherwise a_1 would equal\n    37\t1, contradicting distinctness if k ≥ 2 and one of the others equals 1), and a_1 ∈ {1, ..., n}.\n    38\tHence n/g = 1, i.e., g = n.  But then a_i is divisible by n for every i, forcing a_i = n.\n    39\tThat contradicts distinctness of the a_i.  This is the desired contradiction.\n    40\t\n    41\tStep 7.  Therefore the assumption in Step 1 was false: n does NOT divide a_k(a_1 - 1).\n    42\t```\n    43\t\n    44\t## Key Claims\n    45\t\n    46\t[C1] In Step 1, the cyclic hypothesis n | a_i(a_{i+1} - 1) for all i mod k follows from assuming n | a_k(a_1 - 1) combined with the original hypothesis.\n    47\t\n    48\t[C2] In Step 3, the equation v_p(a_i) + v_p(a_{i+1} - 1) = v is automatic and yields no new information.\n    49\t\n    50\t[C3] In Step 4, the values gcd(a_i, n) form a divisibility chain g_1 | g_2 | ... | g_k | g_1, hence are all equal.\n    51\t\n    52\t[C4] In Step 5, the relation n | a_i(a_{i+1} - 1) implies (n/g) | a_{i+1} - 1, using coprimality of m_i with n/g.\n    53\t\n    54\t[C5] In Step 6, the conclusion a_1 ≡ 1 (mod n/g) combined with a_1 ∈ {1, ..., n} forces n/g = 1.\n    55\t\n    56\t[C6] In Step 6, g = n forces a_i = n for every i, contradicting distinctness.\n    57\t\n    58\t[C7] The proof's final conclusion in Step 7 follows by the contradiction reductio established in Step 6.\n    59\t\n    60\t## Editor's Note\n    61\t\n    62\tThis is the official IMO 2009 Problem 1 solution as presented in the IMO Compendium. Verify each numbered key claim against the solution text, identify the central technique, and assign a rigor score per the audit rubric.\n     8\t\n     9\t## Problem\n    10\t\n    11\tThe 2010 positive numbers \\(a_1, a_2, \\ldots, a_{2010}\\) satisfy the inequality \\(a_i a_j \\leq i + j\\) for all distinct indices \\(i, j\\). Determine, with proof, the largest possible value of the product \\(a_1 a_2 \\cdots a_{2010}\\).\n    12\t\n    13\t## Full Published Solution\n    14\t\n    15\t```text\n    16\tStep 1.  We claim the maximum is achieved when a_i = sqrt(2i - 1) for each i, and the maximum\n    17\tvalue of the product is the product of sqrt(2i - 1) for i = 1, ..., 2010.\n    18\t\n    19\tStep 2.  First, verify that the choice a_i = sqrt(2i - 1) satisfies the constraint a_i · a_j ≤ i + j\n    20\tfor i ≠ j.  We compute a_i · a_j = sqrt((2i - 1)(2j - 1)) = sqrt(4ij - 2i - 2j + 1).\n    21\tWe need to show sqrt(4ij - 2i - 2j + 1) ≤ i + j, i.e., 4ij - 2i - 2j + 1 ≤ (i + j)^2 = i^2 + 2ij + j^2.\n    22\tRearranging: 0 ≤ i^2 - 2ij + j^2 + 2i + 2j - 1 = (i - j)^2 + 2(i + j) - 1.  Since i ≠ j, (i-j)^2 ≥ 1,\n    23\tand 2(i+j) - 1 ≥ 3 (since i + j ≥ 1 + 2 = 3 with i ≠ j and i, j ≥ 1).  So the RHS is positive,\n    24\tand the inequality holds.\n    25\t\n    26\tStep 3.  Now we show no choice can do better.  Consider any valid configuration (a_1, ..., a_{2010})\n    27\tsatisfying a_i a_j ≤ i + j for all i ≠ j.  We will show prod a_i ≤ prod sqrt(2i - 1).\n    28\t\n    29\tStep 4.  By the AM-GM inequality applied to consecutive pairs, a_i · a_{i+1} ≤ 2i + 1, so\n    30\ta_i^2 · a_{i+1}^2 ≤ (2i + 1)^2.  Thus a_i · a_{i+1} ≤ 2i + 1 for every i.\n    31\t\n    32\tStep 5.  Multiplying these constraints for i = 1, 3, 5, ..., 2009 (taking non-overlapping pairs):\n    33\ta_1 a_2 ≤ 3, a_3 a_4 ≤ 7, a_5 a_6 ≤ 11, ..., a_{2009} a_{2010} ≤ 4019.\n    34\tThe product is at most 3 · 7 · 11 · ... · 4019 = prod_{i=1}^{1005} (4i - 1).\n    35\t\n    36\tStep 6.  We claim prod_{i=1}^{2010} sqrt(2i - 1) ≤ prod_{i=1}^{1005} (4i - 1).\n    37\tSquaring both sides: prod_{i=1}^{2010} (2i - 1) ≤ prod_{i=1}^{1005} (4i - 1)^2.\n    38\tThe LHS is the product of odd integers from 1 to 4019; the RHS expands accordingly.  Direct\n    39\tcomputation confirms the inequality (left as exercise).\n    40\t\n    41\tStep 7.  Combining: prod a_i ≤ prod_{i=1}^{1005} (4i - 1) ≤ prod_{i=1}^{2010} sqrt(2i - 1),\n    42\tso the maximum is prod sqrt(2i - 1) as claimed.\n    43\t```\n    44\t\n    45\t## Key Claims\n    46\t\n    47\t[C1] In Step 2, the algebraic check that (i - j)^2 + 2(i + j) - 1 > 0 for distinct positive integers i, j establishes that a_i = sqrt(2i - 1) satisfies the constraint.\n    48\t\n    49\t[C2] In Step 4, the inequality a_i · a_{i+1} ≤ 2i + 1 follows directly from substituting i and j = i + 1 into the constraint a_i a_j ≤ i + j.\n    50\t\n    51\t[C3] In Step 5, taking non-overlapping consecutive pairs gives prod a_i ≤ 3 · 7 · 11 · ... · 4019, the product of (4i - 1) for i = 1 to 1005.\n    52\t\n    53\t[C4] In Step 6, the claim prod (2i - 1) for i=1 to 2010 ≤ prod (4i - 1)^2 for i = 1 to 1005 is established by direct computation.\n    54\t\n    55\t[C5] In Step 7, combining the upper bound from Step 5 with the inequality in Step 6 produces the final bound prod sqrt(2i - 1).\n    56\t\n    57\t[C6] The maximum value claimed in Step 1 (product of sqrt(2i - 1) over i = 1 to 2010) is achieved by the specific configuration a_i = sqrt(2i - 1).\n    58\t\n    59\t## Editor's Note\n    60\t\n    61\tThis is the USAMO 2010 Problem 3 official solution. Check 2 | 2^{2-1} - 1 = 2^1 - 1 = 1: 2 does not divide 1, so the\n    27\tfirst condition fails for p = 2.  Therefore p = 2 is excluded.\n    28\t\n    29\tStep 6. Therefore A is a king, completing the proof.\n    31\t\n    32\tStep 7. Examples: a sign error in a derived equation, a misuse of a theorem (precondition unverified AND consequence is wrong), a counterexample to the stated claim.
